    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I came in because... well, my wife made the appointment, honestly. But I guess I'm here because nothing feels like mine anymore. I used to get excited about things, you know? Like, I used to love checking out the new murals downtown. There's this one guy, he paints these massive, colorful birds on the sides of old brick buildings. Really beautiful work. I think he uses spray paint, mostly, but the detail is incredible. I saw him working once, up on a cherry picker... Anyway, I used to love that stuff. Or going for runs, playing pickup basketball on the weekends. Even just making a really good drink at work—I've been a bartender for twenty-something years, and I used to take pride in mixing a perfect Old Fashioned. Now? I just stand behind the bar like I don't care about any of it. The bottles, the glasses, the people talking... it's just noise. My buddy Dave came in the other night, ordered his usual, and I couldn't even muster a smile. Dave's a good guy, works over at the auto shop... but yeah, I just don't care about anything I used to.

It's been... I'd say about two months now. Yeah, right around the time the bar got bought out. I mean, twenty years I worked there, helping build the place up, getting the regulars in, and then these new guys come in and just... change everything. They even changed the lighting. Used to be this nice, warm amber kind of glow, you know? Really made people want to sit and stay a while. Now it's all these harsh, bright LED things that make everyone look like they haven't slept in a week. And they got rid of the old jukebox. That thing was a classic. Had real records in it. I remember when we had to get a guy out from three towns over just to fix the coin slot on it... took him like four hours, but he got it working. But yeah, since the buyout, everything I worked toward is just... gone. That's when this all started.

No. Honestly, no. I was just saying this to my wife the other day... or maybe it was Dave, I don't remember. But like I told you, I used to love those murals, going for runs, shooting hoops. Even just mixing a drink used to feel good. And now... nothing. I just stand there at work and I don't care about a single bit of it. It's like someone flipped a switch. Speaking of switches, the new owners put in this whole automated tap system. It's supposed to pour the perfect pint every time, but half the time it just foams up over the glass and makes a mess. I told them they needed to adjust the pressure on the kegs—because, you know, depending on the temperature in the cooler, the CO2 levels can get all messed up. I used to manage all that stuff myself. I'd go down to the cellar, check the gauges, make sure everything was running smooth. It was quiet down there, just the hum of the compressors... but yeah, to answer your question, no. I don't enjoy anything anymore.

S029\n  name: Fatigue\n  description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: By the time I carry a couple cases of liquor or set up the well, my body feels like I've already worked a double, even if I barely did anything that day.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep thinking I let down the younger bartenders and the artists I promised space to, and it manifestation: Inventory used to be automatic for me, but now I stare at the bottles and lose count three times, or I freeze trying to decide something simple like what garnish to restock.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the major depressive episode\n  manifestation: Before I sank into this I also stopped seeing friends for runs, basketball, or mural shoots because I can't make myself show up.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about two months, since the bar was bought out and everything I had been working toward got changed.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / bartender / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S025\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because... well, my wife made the appointment, honestly. But I guess I'm here because nothing feels like mine anymore. I used to get excited about things, you know? Like, I used to love checking out the new murals downtown. There's this one guy, he paints these massive, colorful birds on the sides of old brick buildings. Really beautiful work. I think he uses spray paint, mostly, but the detail is incredible. I saw him working once, up on a cherry picker... Anyway, I used to love that stuff. Or going for runs, playing pickup basketball on the weekends. Even just making a really good drink at work—I've been a bartender for twenty-something years, and I used to take pride in mixing a perfect Old Fashioned. Now? I just stand behind the bar like I don't care about any of it. The bottles, the glasses, the people talking... it's just noise. My buddy Dave came in the other night, ordered his usual, and I couldn't even muster a smile. Speaking of switches, the new owners put in this whole automated tap system. It's supposed to pour the perfect pint every time, but half the time it just foams up over the glass and makes a mess. I told them they needed to adjust the pressure on the kegs—because, you know, depending on the temperature in the cooler, the CO2 levels can get all messed up. I used to manage all that stuff myself. I'd go down to the cellar, check the gauges, make sure everything was running smooth. It was quiet down there, just the hum of the compressors... but yeah, to answer your question, no. I don't enjoy anything anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost interest in activities that you normally found pleasurable?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I don't know if sad is the right word for it. I'm not really sure. My wife actually asked me the exact same thing yesterday when she came home with this new sweater for me. She said the color was supposed to cheer me up or something. It's this bright blue... and I haven't worn anything like that since the new owners made us switch our uniforms at work. 

Did I mention the new uniforms? They've got us in these stiff, black button-downs now with their new logo embroidered right on the chest. The collar is so stiff it practically chokes you, and the fabric doesn't breathe at all. I mean, you're running around on a busy Friday night, hauling thirty-pound ice buckets from the back, and you're just sweating straight through this cheap polyester. The old owner, Mike, he used to just let us wear whatever black t-shirt we wanted as long as it didn't have holes in it and was clean. Made it so much easier to actually move around and do the job. You can't reach up to the top shelf for the good bourbon in these new shirts without feeling like you're gonna rip a seam. 

But, uh... what were you asking? Oh, sad. Yeah, I don't really know about sad. I just haven't really noticed feeling much of anything.

I feel like we're just going in circles here. I mean, I told you, right? I came in because nothing feels like mine anymore. I used to actually get excited about things. I mean, I used to love those things. The murals, playing basketball, going for a run... even just standing behind the bar and mixing a really good Old Fashioned. I used to take pride in it. Which reminds me, the other bartender they hired, this kid named Tyler? He doesn't even know how to properly muddle an orange peel. I watched him just smash it to a pulp the other night. They're more worried about their new fancy POS system. The screen is so bright it hurts my eyes, and half the time it doesn't even send the tickets to the kitchen right. They've got us in these stiff, black button-downs now with their new logo embroidered right on the chest. The collar is so stiff it practically chokes you, and the fabric doesn't breathe at all. The cardboard is so thin now that if you try to pick them up by the sides, the bottom just falls right out. We lost three bottles of gin last week because of that. Just shattered everywhere. The smell of juniper was stuck in the floor mats for three days, no matter how many times I mopped it with the heavy-duty degreaser. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't know exactly how much weight, but yeah, my pants are definitely looser. Most days I just forget to eat entirely until my hands start shaking while I'm trying to pour a drink behind the bar. Then maybe I'll grab a few bites of whatever cold fries or something are left after we close, but even just looking at it makes me feel sick. 

Which is a shame, because the kitchen staff, well, the old kitchen staff, they used to make this incredible family meal before service. Carlos, the old head chef, he'd do these slow-roasted pork tacos on Thursdays. The smell would just fill the whole prep area. He'd marinate the pork for like two days in this citrus and achiote blend. But Carlos left when the new owners took over. Said they were trying to cut his food costs so much he couldn't even buy decent tomatoes anymore. Now they've got this guy, Greg, running the line. Greg doesn't even taste the food before it goes out. I saw him send out a plate of wings the other night that were practically still frozen in the middle. And he's always blasting this terrible techno music from a little Bluetooth speaker right next to the fryers. You can hear it all the way out in the dining room when the kitchen doors swing open. It totally ruins the vibe. We used to just have low-key jazz or something playing, you know? Kept people relaxed. 

But yeah, I haven't really been eating much. Just don't have the stomach for it.

Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. All the time, honestly. I keep thinking about how I let down the younger bartenders. And the artists, too. I promised them I'd secure some wall space for their murals before the new owners came in, and I just... I didn't get it done in time. Now they have nowhere to show their work. I wrecked their shot, and it just feels like I deserve whatever happens to me because of it. 

Speaking of the murals, you should have seen what this one kid, Leo, was working on. He had these massive canvases in his garage. He uses this technique where he mixes sand into the paint to give it texture, and he was doing this whole series on the history of the neighborhood. The colors were just unbelievable. Really vibrant reds and these deep, rich ochres. He said he had to order the pigments special from some supplier in New York. I remember he was telling me about how the shipping costs were killing him, but he refused to compromise on the quality. That's the thing about these young artists, they just pour everything they have into it. Leo was working two jobs just to afford the materials. He'd come into the bar after his shift at the warehouse, completely exhausted, with paint still stuck under his fingernails, and just talk for hours about color theory and composition. I used to give him a free soda and just listen. 

But, uh, yeah... I just feel like it's all my fault. I really let them down.

The smell of juniper was stuck in the floor mats for three days, no matter how many times I mopped it with the heavy-duty degreaser. No, no, I haven't been thinking about hurting myself. I just... I don't know, I just feel empty, mostly. Not like I want to do anything bad to myself. 

Though, you know, it is dangerous behind the bar sometimes. People don't realize. Just last month, before I really started feeling this way, we had this guy come in, completely out of his mind. He was swinging a pool cue around because someone bumped into him near the jukebox. The jukebox is this vintage Wurlitzer, beautiful machine, it lights up with all these neon tubes, pinks and greens. The owner bought it at an auction in Chicago, paid a fortune to have it shipped down here and restored. It still takes actual quarters, none of that digital app nonsense. You have to physically push the heavy metal buttons, and you can hear the mechanism whirring and clanking as it grabs the record. Anyway, this guy swings the cue and smashes right into the glass of the Wurlitzer. Glass went everywhere, all over the floor, all over the guy who bumped him. I had to jump over the bar and physically tackle the guy with the cue before he hit someone. My knee still aches from where I landed on the brass footrail. 

But no... I'm not thinking about hurting myself. Just... just tired.

That's when this all started.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy anything at all, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: No. Honestly, no. I was just saying this to my wife the other day... or maybe it was Dave, I don't remember. But like I told you, I used to love those murals, going for runs, shooting hoops. Even just mixing a drink used to feel good. And now... nothing. I just stand there at work and I don't care about a single bit of it. It's like someone flipped a switch. Speaking of switches, the new owners put in this whole automated tap system. It's supposed to pour the perfect pint every time, but half the time it just foams up over the glass and makes a mess. I told them they needed to adjust the pressure on the kegs—because, you know, depending on the temperature in the cooler, the CO2 levels can get all messed up. I used to manage all that stuff myself. I'd go down to the cellar, check the gauges, make sure everything was running smooth. It was quiet down there, just the hum of the compressors... but yeah, to answer your question, no. I don't enjoy anything anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you normally found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I feel like I just said this, but yeah. I mean, I used to love those things. The murals, playing basketball, going for a run... even just standing behind the bar and mixing a really good Old Fashioned. I used to take pride in it. Now I just stand there and I don't care about any of it. Nothing feels like mine anymore. It's like I'm just watching someone else do my job. Which reminds me, the other bartender they hired, this kid named Tyler? He doesn't even know how to properly muddle an orange peel. I watched him just smash it to a pulp the other night. You're supposed to just express the oils, you know? Just a light twist over the glass, maybe rub it on the rim. But he just crushes it. And the new owners don't even care. They're more worried about their new fancy POS system. The screen is so bright it hurts my eyes, and half the time it doesn't even send the tickets to the kitchen right. We had a guy wait forty-five minutes for a burger on Tuesday because the ticket just vanished into thin air... but yeah, I don't enjoy anything. At all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don't know if sad is the right word for it. I'm not really sure. My wife actually asked me the exact same thing yesterday when she came home with this new sweater for me. She said the color was supposed to cheer me up or something. It's this bright blue... and I haven't worn anything like that since the new owners made us switch our uniforms at work. \n\nDid I mention the new uniforms? They've got us in these stiff, black button-downs now with their new logo embroidered right on the chest. The collar is so stiff it practically chokes you, and the fabric doesn't breathe at all. I mean, you're running around on a busy Friday night, hauling thirty-pound ice buckets from the back, and you're just sweating straight through this cheap polyester. The old owner, Mike, he used to just let us wear whatever black t-shirt we wanted as long as it didn't have holes in it and was clean. Made it so much easier to actually move around and do the job. You can't reach up to the top shelf for the good bourbon in these new shirts without feeling like you're gonna rip a seam. \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, sad. Yeah, I don't really know about sad. I just haven't really noticed feeling much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure when you're doing things, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're just going in circles here. I mean, I told you, right? I came in because nothing feels like mine anymore. I used to actually get excited about things. The murals, going for runs, playing basketball... even just making a really good drink at work. Now I just stand there and it's like I don't care about any of it. \n\nSpeaking of not caring, the delivery guy today, he just dumped all the new liquor boxes right in the middle of the hallway. Didn't even bother stacking them against the wall. I almost tripped over a case of vodka on my way to the cooler. And the boxes are getting cheaper, too. The cardboard is so thin now that if you try to pick them up by the sides, the bottom just falls right out. We lost three bottles of gin last week because of that. Just shattered everywhere. The smell of juniper was stuck in the floor mats for three days, no matter how many times I mopped it with the heavy-duty degreaser. We used to get the good citrus degreaser, the one that actually cut through the stickiness, but now they buy this cheap industrial stuff that just smells like bleach and gives me a headache. \n\nBut yeah... no pleasure. None at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don't know exactly how much weight, but yeah, my pants are definitely looser. Most days I just forget to eat entirely until my hands start shaking while I'm trying to pour a drink behind the bar. Then maybe I'll grab a few bites of whatever cold fries or something are left after we close, but even just looking at it makes me feel sick. \n\nWhich is a shame, because the kitchen staff, well, the old kitchen staff, they used to make this incredible family meal before service. Carlos, the old head chef, he'd do these slow-roasted pork tacos on Thursdays. The smell would just fill the whole prep area. He'd marinate the pork for like two days in this citrus and achiote blend. But Carlos left when the new owners took over. Said they were trying to cut his food costs so much he couldn't even buy decent tomatoes anymore. Now they've got this guy, Greg, running the line. Greg doesn't even taste the food before it goes out. I saw him send out a plate of wings the other night that were practically still frozen in the middle. And he's always blasting this terrible techno music from a little Bluetooth speaker right next to the fryers. You can hear it all the way out in the dining room when the kitchen doors swing open. It totally ruins the vibe. We used to just have low-key jazz or something playing, you know? Kept people relaxed. \n\nBut yeah, I haven't really been eating much. Just don't have the stomach for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. All the time, honestly. I keep thinking about how I let down the younger bartenders. And the artists, too. I promised them I'd secure some wall space for their murals before the new owners came in, and I just... I didn't get it done in time. Now they have nowhere to show their work. I wrecked their shot, and it just feels like I deserve whatever happens to me because of it. \n\nSpeaking of the murals, you should have seen what this one kid, Leo, was working on. He had these massive canvases in his garage. He uses this technique where he mixes sand into the paint to give it texture, and he was doing this whole series on the history of the neighborhood. The colors were just unbelievable. Really vibrant reds and these deep, rich ochres. He said he had to order the pigments special from some supplier in New York. I remember he was telling me about how the shipping costs were killing him, but he refused to compromise on the quality. That's the thing about these young artists, they just pour everything they have into it. Leo was working two jobs just to afford the materials. He'd come into the bar after his shift at the warehouse, completely exhausted, with paint still stuck under his fingernails, and just talk for hours about color theory and composition. I used to give him a free soda and just listen. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... I just feel like it's all my fault. I really let them down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't been thinking about hurting myself. I just... I don't know, I just feel empty, mostly. Not like I want to do anything bad to myself. \n\nThough, you know, it is dangerous behind the bar sometimes. People don't realize. Just last month, before I really started feeling this way, we had this guy come in, completely out of his mind. He was swinging a pool cue around because someone bumped into him near the jukebox. The jukebox is this vintage Wurlitzer, beautiful machine, it lights up with all these neon tubes, pinks and greens. The owner bought it at an auction in Chicago, paid a fortune to have it shipped down here and restored. It still takes actual quarters, none of that digital app nonsense. You have to physically push the heavy metal buttons, and you can hear the mechanism whirring and clanking as it grabs the record. Anyway, this guy swings the cue and smashes right into the glass of the Wurlitzer. Glass went everywhere, all over the floor, all over the guy who bumped him. I had to jump over the bar and physically tackle the guy with the cue before he hit someone. My knee still aches from where I landed on the brass footrail. \n\nBut no... I'm not thinking about hurting myself. Just... just tired.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don't know exactly how much weight, but yeah, my pants are definitely looser. Most days I just forget to eat entirely until my hands start shaking while I'm trying to pour a drink behind the bar. Then maybe I'll grab a few bites of whatever cold fries or something are left after we close, but even just looking at it makes me feel sick. \n\nWhich is a shame, because the kitchen staff, well, the old kitchen staff, they used to make this incredible family meal before service. Carlos, the old head chef, he'd do these slow-roasted pork tacos on Thursdays. The smell would just fill the whole prep area. He'd marinate the pork for like two days in this citrus and achiote blend. But Carlos left when the new owners took over. Said they were trying to cut his food costs so much he couldn't even buy decent tomatoes anymore. Now they've got this guy, Greg, running the line. Greg doesn't even taste the food before it goes out. I saw him send out a plate of wings the other night that were practically still frozen in the middle. And he's always blasting this terrible techno music from a little Bluetooth speaker right next to the fryers. You can hear it all the way out in the dining room when the kitchen doors swing open. It totally ruins the vibe. We used to just have low-key jazz or something playing, you know? Kept people relaxed. \n\nBut yeah, I haven't really been eating much. Just don't have the stomach for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. All the time, honestly. I keep thinking about how I let down the younger bartenders. And the artists, too. I promised them I'd secure some wall space for their murals before the new owners came in, and I just... I didn't get it done in time. Now they have nowhere to show their work. I wrecked their shot, and it just feels like I deserve whatever happens to me because of it. \n\nSpeaking of the murals, you should have seen what this one kid, Leo, was working on. He had these massive canvases in his garage. He uses this technique where he mixes sand into the paint to give it texture, and he was doing this whole series on the history of the neighborhood. The colors were just unbelievable. Really vibrant reds and these deep, rich ochres. He said he had to order the pigments special from some supplier in New York. I remember he was telling me about how the shipping costs were killing him, but he refused to compromise on the quality. That's the thing about these young artists, they just pour everything they have into it. Leo was working two jobs just to afford the materials. He'd come into the bar after his shift at the warehouse, completely exhausted, with paint still stuck under his fingernails, and just talk for hours about color theory and composition. I used to give him a free soda and just listen. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... I just feel like it's all my fault. I really let them down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't been thinking about hurting myself. I just... I don't know, I just feel empty, mostly. Not like I want to do anything bad to myself. \n\nThough, you know, it is dangerous behind the bar sometimes. People don't realize. The jukebox is this vintage Wurlitzer, beautiful machine, it lights up with all these neon tubes, pinks and greens. The owner bought it at an auction in Chicago, paid a fortune to have it shipped down here and restored. It still takes actual quarters, none of that digital app nonsense. You have to physically push the heavy metal buttons, and you can hear the mechanism whirring and clanking as it grabs the record. Anyway, this guy swings the cue and smashes right into the glass of the Wurlitzer. Glass went everywhere, all over the floor, all over the guy who bumped him. I had to jump over the bar and physically tackle the guy with the cue before he hit someone. My knee still aches from where I landed on the brass footrail. \n\nBut no... I'm not thinking about hurting myself. Just... just tired.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that you've lost interest in almost all activities that you used to enjoy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / bartender / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"I came in because nothing feels like mine anymore—I used to get excited about murals, runs, basketball, even making a good drink, and now I just stand there like I don't care about any of it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah... yeah, I guess so. I actually came in because nothing feels like mine anymore, you know? I used to get so excited about things. Helping the kids with their murals, going for runs in the morning, playing pick-up basketball on Sundays... hell, even just making a really good, balanced cocktail used to give me a little thrill. Now I just stand behind the bar and I don't care about any of it. 

Speaking of basketball, the courts over at the park are just falling apart. It's a shame. The city was supposed to resurface them last spring, but they just patched a few of the worst cracks with this cheap asphalt filler that gets all sticky when the sun beats down on it. And the nets! They haven't replaced the nets in years. Half of them are just hanging by a single loop of chain, clanking against the rim every time the wind blows. I remember back in the day, we used to have these huge tournaments out there. Teams from all over the city would show up. There was this one guy, Marcus, he was maybe six-foot-four, played D2 ball somewhere up state. He had this fadeaway jumper that was just impossible to block. He'd always wear these bright neon green sneakers, said they distracted the defense. I don't know if it worked, but nobody could guard him. We used to play until the streetlights came on, and then we'd all head over to the corner bodega for Gatorades and cheap pizza. 

But yeah... I just don't have the energy or the care for any of it anymore. I just feel totally disconnected from it all.

I used to get excited about things, you know? Like, I used to love checking out the new murals downtown. There's this one guy, he paints these massive, colorful birds on the sides of old brick buildings. Really beautiful work. I think he uses spray paint, mostly, but the detail is incredible. I saw him working once, up on a cherry picker... Anyway, I used to love that stuff. Or going for runs, playing pickup basketball on the weekends. Even just making a really good drink at work—I've been a bartender for twenty-something years, and I used to take pride in mixing a perfect Old Fashioned. Now? I just stand behind the bar like I don't care about any of it. The bottles, the glasses, the people talking... it's just noise. My buddy Dave came in the other night, ordered his usual, and I couldn't even muster a smile. Dave's a good guy, works over at the auto shop... but yeah, I just don't care about anything I used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, like nothing feels like yours anymore?\n\nPatient: It's been... I'd say about two months now. Yeah, right around the time the bar got bought out. I mean, twenty years I worked there, helping build the place up, getting the regulars in, and then these new guys come in and just... change everything. They even changed the lighting. Used to be this nice, warm amber kind of glow, you know? Really made people want to sit and stay a while. Now it's all these harsh, bright LED things that make everyone look like they haven't slept in a week. And they got rid of the old jukebox. That thing was a classic. Had real records in it. I remember when we had to get a guy out from three towns over just to fix the coin slot on it... took him like four hours, but he got it working. But yeah, since the buyout, everything I worked toward is just... gone. Even just mixing a drink used to feel good. And now... nothing. I just stand there at work and I don't care about a single bit of it. It's like someone flipped a switch. Speaking of switches, the new owners put in this whole automated tap system. It's supposed to pour the perfect pint every time, but half the time it just foams up over the glass and makes a mess. I told them they needed to adjust the pressure on the kegs—because, you know, depending on the temperature in the cooler, the CO2 levels can get all messed up. I used to manage all that stuff myself. I'd go down to the cellar, check the gauges, make sure everything was running smooth. It was quiet down there, just the hum of the compressors... but yeah, to answer your question, no. I don't enjoy anything anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you normally found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I feel like I just said this, but yeah. I mean, I used to love those things. The murals, playing basketball, going for a run... even just standing behind the bar and mixing a really good Old Fashioned. I used to take pride in it. Now I just stand there and I don't care about any of it. Nothing feels like mine anymore. It's like I'm just watching someone else do my job. Which reminds me, the other bartender they hired, this kid named Tyler? He doesn't even know how to properly muddle an orange peel. I watched him just smash it to a pulp the other night. You're supposed to just express the oils, you know? Just a light twist over the glass, maybe rub it on the rim. But he just crushes it. And the new owners don't even care. They're more worried about their new fancy POS system. The screen is so bright it hurts my eyes, and half the time it doesn't even send the tickets to the kitchen right. We had a guy wait forty-five minutes for a burger on Tuesday because the ticket just vanished into thin air... but yeah, I don't enjoy anything. At all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don't know if sad is the right word for it. I'm not really sure. My wife actually asked me the exact same thing yesterday when she came home with this new sweater for me. She said the color was supposed to cheer me up or something. It's this bright blue... and I haven't worn anything like that since the new owners made us switch our uniforms at work. \n\nDid I mention the new uniforms? They've got us in these stiff, black button-downs now with their new logo embroidered right on the chest. The collar is so stiff it practically chokes you, and the fabric doesn't breathe at all. The old owner, Mike, he used to just let us wear whatever black t-shirt we wanted as long as it didn't have holes in it and was clean. Made it so much easier to actually move around and do the job. You can't reach up to the top shelf for the good bourbon in these new shirts without feeling like you're gonna rip a seam. \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, sad. Yeah, I don't really know about sad. I just haven't really noticed feeling much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure when you're doing things, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're just going in circles here. Now I just stand there and it's like I don't care about any of it. \n\nSpeaking of not caring, the delivery guy today, he just dumped all the new liquor boxes right in the middle of the hallway. Didn't even bother stacking them against the wall. I almost tripped over a case of vodka on my way to the cooler. And the boxes are getting cheaper, too. The cardboard is so thin now that if you try to pick them up by the sides, the bottom just falls right out. We lost three bottles of gin last week because of that. Just shattered everywhere. The smell of juniper was stuck in the floor mats for three days, no matter how many times I mopped it with the heavy-duty degreaser. We used to get the good citrus degreaser, the one that actually cut through the stickiness, but now they buy this cheap industrial stuff that just smells like bleach and gives me a headache. \n\nBut yeah... no pleasure. None at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don't know exactly how much weight, but yeah, my pants are definitely looser. Just don't have the stomach for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. All the time, honestly. I keep thinking about how I let down the younger bartenders. And the artists, too. I promised them I'd secure some wall space for their murals before the new owners came in, and I just... I didn't get it done in time. Now they have nowhere to show their work. I wrecked their shot, and it just feels like I deserve whatever happens to me because of it. \n\nSpeaking of the murals, you should have seen what this one kid, Leo, was working on. He had these massive canvases in his garage. He uses this technique where he mixes sand into the paint to give it texture, and he was doing this whole series on the history of the neighborhood. The colors were just unbelievable. Really vibrant reds and these deep, rich ochres. He said he had to order the pigments special from some supplier in New York. I remember he was telling me about how the shipping costs were killing him, but he refused to compromise on the quality. I used to give him a free soda and just listen. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... I just feel like it's all my fault. I really let them down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't been thinking about hurting myself. I just... The jukebox is this vintage Wurlitzer, beautiful machine, it lights up with all these neon tubes, pinks and greens. The owner bought it at an auction in Chicago, paid a fortune to have it shipped down here and restored. It still takes actual quarters, none of that digital app nonsense. You have to physically push the heavy metal buttons, and you can hear the mechanism whirring and clanking as it grabs the record. Anyway, this guy swings the cue and smashes right into the glass of the Wurlitzer. Glass went everywhere, all over the floor, all over the guy who bumped him. I'm not thinking about hurting myself. Just... just tired.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've lost interest in almost all activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I guess so. Yeah, sleep is... it's really messed up right now. I get home and I'm exhausted, you know? Just completely drained. So I lie down, but my head just starts spinning. It just keeps going over every single mistake I made at work, every little thing I messed up. And then when I finally do pass out, I'm wide awake by four or five in the morning and I just can't get back to sleep. 

It's so quiet at that time of morning. I usually end up just staring out the window. You can see the bakery across the street starting up their ovens. They get their flour deliveries right around 4:30. This massive truck pulls up—it's one of those big eighteen-wheelers, always has this loud, screeching air brake that echoes off all the buildings. The driver is this older guy, must be in his sixties, always wears a faded Mets cap. He unloads these huge fifty-pound sacks of flour on a hand truck, just stacks them up on the sidewalk before hauling them inside. Sometimes the bakery owner comes out and hands him a coffee and one of those fresh croissants they make. They make the best almond croissants there, really flaky, with powdered sugar all over the top. I used to go over there and grab one before my morning run, back when I actually did that. The smell of the butter and the yeast just fills the whole street before the sun even comes up. 

But yeah, I just... I lie there and can't go back to sleep. It's miserable.

There's this one guy, he paints these massive, colorful birds on the sides of old brick buildings. Really beautiful work. I think he uses spray paint, mostly, but the detail is incredible. I saw him working once, up on a cherry picker... Anyway, I used to love that stuff. Or going for runs, playing pickup basketball on the weekends. Even just making a really good drink at work—I've been a bartender for twenty-something years, and I used to take pride in mixing a perfect Old Fashioned. Now? I just stand behind the bar like I don't care about any of it. The bottles, the glasses, the people talking... it's just noise. My buddy Dave came in the other night, ordered his usual, and I couldn't even muster a smile. Dave's a good guy, works over at the auto shop... but yeah, I just don't care about anything I used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, like nothing feels like yours anymore?\n\nPatient: It's been... I'd say about two months now. Yeah, right around the time the bar got bought out. I mean, twenty years I worked there, helping build the place up, getting the regulars in, and then these new guys come in and just... change everything. They even changed the lighting. Used to be this nice, warm amber kind of glow, you know? Really made people want to sit and stay a while. Now it's all these harsh, bright LED things that make everyone look like they haven't slept in a week. And they got rid of the old jukebox. That thing was a classic. Had real records in it. I remember when we had to get a guy out from three towns over just to fix the coin slot on it... took him like four hours, but he got it working. But yeah, since the buyout, everything I worked toward is just... gone. That's when this all started.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy anything at all, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: No. Honestly, no. I was just saying this to my wife the other day... or maybe it was Dave, I don't remember. But like I told you, I used to love those murals, going for runs, shooting hoops. Even just mixing a drink used to feel good. And now... nothing. I just stand there at work and I don't care about a single bit of it. It's like someone flipped a switch. Speaking of switches, the new owners put in this whole automated tap system. It's supposed to pour the perfect pint every time, but half the time it just foams up over the glass and makes a mess. I told them they needed to adjust the pressure on the kegs—because, you know, depending on the temperature in the cooler, the CO2 levels can get all messed up. I used to manage all that stuff myself. I'd go down to the cellar, check the gauges, make sure everything was running smooth. It was quiet down there, just the hum of the compressors... but yeah, to answer your question, no. I don't enjoy anything anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you normally found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I feel like I just said this, but yeah. I mean, I used to love those things. The murals, playing basketball, going for a run... even just standing behind the bar and mixing a really good Old Fashioned. I used to take pride in it. Now I just stand there and I don't care about any of it. Nothing feels like mine anymore. It's like I'm just watching someone else do my job. Which reminds me, the other bartender they hired, this kid named Tyler? He doesn't even know how to properly muddle an orange peel. I watched him just smash it to a pulp the other night. You're supposed to just express the oils, you know? Just a light twist over the glass, maybe rub it on the rim. But he just crushes it. And the new owners don't even care. They're more worried about their new fancy POS system. The screen is so bright it hurts my eyes, and half the time it doesn't even send the tickets to the kitchen right. We had a guy wait forty-five minutes for a burger on Tuesday because the ticket just vanished into thin air... but yeah, I don't enjoy anything. At all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don't know if sad is the right word for it. I'm not really sure. My wife actually asked me the exact same thing yesterday when she came home with this new sweater for me. She said the color was supposed to cheer me up or something. It's this bright blue... and I haven't worn anything like that since the new owners made us switch our uniforms at work. \n\nDid I mention the new uniforms? They've got us in these stiff, black button-downs now with their new logo embroidered right on the chest. The collar is so stiff it practically chokes you, and the fabric doesn't breathe at all. I mean, you're running around on a busy Friday night, hauling thirty-pound ice buckets from the back, and you're just sweating straight through this cheap polyester. The old owner, Mike, he used to just let us wear whatever black t-shirt we wanted as long as it didn't have holes in it and was clean. Made it so much easier to actually move around and do the job. You can't reach up to the top shelf for the good bourbon in these new shirts without feeling like you're gonna rip a seam. \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, sad. Yeah, I don't really know about sad. I just haven't really noticed feeling much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure when you're doing things, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're just going in circles here. I mean, I told you, right? I came in because nothing feels like mine anymore. I used to actually get excited about things. The murals, going for runs, playing basketball... even just making a really good drink at work. Now I just stand there and it's like I don't care about any of it. \n\nSpeaking of not caring, the delivery guy today, he just dumped all the new liquor boxes right in the middle of the hallway. Didn't even bother stacking them against the wall. I almost tripped over a case of vodka on my way to the cooler. And the boxes are getting cheaper, too. The cardboard is so thin now that if you try to pick them up by the sides, the bottom just falls right out. We lost three bottles of gin last week because of that. Just shattered everywhere. The smell of juniper was stuck in the floor mats for three days, no matter how many times I mopped it with the heavy-duty degreaser. We used to get the good citrus degreaser, the one that actually cut through the stickiness, but now they buy this cheap industrial stuff that just smells like bleach and gives me a headache. \n\nBut yeah... no pleasure. None at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don't know exactly how much weight, but yeah, my pants are definitely looser. Most days I just forget to eat entirely until my hands start shaking while I'm trying to pour a drink behind the bar. Then maybe I'll grab a few bites of whatever cold fries or something are left after we close, but even just looking at it makes me feel sick. \n\nWhich is a shame, because the kitchen staff, well, the old kitchen staff, they used to make this incredible family meal before service. Carlos, the old head chef, he'd do these slow-roasted pork tacos on Thursdays. The smell would just fill the whole prep area. He'd marinate the pork for like two days in this citrus and achiote blend. But Carlos left when the new owners took over. Said they were trying to cut his food costs so much he couldn't even buy decent tomatoes anymore. Now they've got this guy, Greg, running the line. Greg doesn't even taste the food before it goes out. I saw him send out a plate of wings the other night that were practically still frozen in the middle. And he's always blasting this terrible techno music from a little Bluetooth speaker right next to the fryers. You can hear it all the way out in the dining room when the kitchen doors swing open. It totally ruins the vibe. We used to just have low-key jazz or something playing, you know? Kept people relaxed. \n\nBut yeah, I haven't really been eating much. Just don't have the stomach for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. All the time, honestly. I keep thinking about how I let down the younger bartenders. And the artists, too. I promised them I'd secure some wall space for their murals before the new owners came in, and I just... I didn't get it done in time. Now they have nowhere to show their work. I wrecked their shot, and it just feels like I deserve whatever happens to me because of it. \n\nSpeaking of the murals, you should have seen what this one kid, Leo, was working on. He had these massive canvases in his garage. He uses this technique where he mixes sand into the paint to give it texture, and he was doing this whole series on the history of the neighborhood. The colors were just unbelievable. Really vibrant reds and these deep, rich ochres. He said he had to order the pigments special from some supplier in New York. I remember he was telling me about how the shipping costs were killing him, but he refused to compromise on the quality. That's the thing about these young artists, they just pour everything they have into it. Leo was working two jobs just to afford the materials. He'd come into the bar after his shift at the warehouse, completely exhausted, with paint still stuck under his fingernails, and just talk for hours about color theory and composition. I used to give him a free soda and just listen. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... I just feel like it's all my fault. I really let them down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't been thinking about hurting myself. I just... I don't know, I just feel empty, mostly. Not like I want to do anything bad to myself. \n\nThough, you know, it is dangerous behind the bar sometimes. People don't realize. Just last month, before I really started feeling this way, we had this guy come in, completely out of his mind. He was swinging a pool cue around because someone bumped into him near the jukebox. The jukebox is this vintage Wurlitzer, beautiful machine, it lights up with all these neon tubes, pinks and greens. The owner bought it at an auction in Chicago, paid a fortune to have it shipped down here and restored. It still takes actual quarters, none of that digital app nonsense. You have to physically push the heavy metal buttons, and you can hear the mechanism whirring and clanking as it grabs the record. Anyway, this guy swings the cue and smashes right into the glass of the Wurlitzer. Glass went everywhere, all over the floor, all over the guy who bumped him. I had to jump over the bar and physically tackle the guy with the cue before he hit someone. My knee still aches from where I landed on the brass footrail. \n\nBut no... I'm not thinking about hurting myself. Just... just tired.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've lost interest in almost all activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I guess so. I actually came in because nothing feels like mine anymore, you know? I used to get so excited about things. Helping the kids with their murals, going for runs in the morning, playing pick-up basketball on Sundays... hell, even just making a really good, balanced cocktail used to give me a little thrill. Now I just stand behind the bar and I don't care about any of it. \n\nSpeaking of basketball, the courts over at the park are just falling apart. It's a shame. They haven't replaced the nets in years. Half of them are just hanging by a single loop of chain, clanking against the rim every time the wind blows. I remember back in the day, we used to have these huge tournaments out there. Teams from all over the city would show up. He'd always wear these bright neon green sneakers, said they distracted the defense. I don't know if it worked, but nobody could guard him. We used to play until the streetlights came on, and then we'd all head over to the corner bodega for Gatorades and cheap pizza. \n\nBut yeah... I just don't have the energy or the care for any of it anymore. I just feel totally disconnected from it all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant changes in your sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep is... it's really messed up right now. I get home and I'm exhausted, you know? Just completely drained. So I lie down, but my head just starts spinning. It just keeps going over every single mistake I made at work, every little thing I messed up. The smell of the butter and the yeast just fills the whole street before the sun even comes up. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I lie there and can't go back to sleep. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, definitely. It's like I have absolutely nothing left in the tank. By the time I carry a couple cases of liquor from the back room or just set up the well for the shift, my body feels like I've already worked a double. And that's even if I barely did anything that day. 

Just moving those cases... we actually just switched to this new local distributor for our well spirits. They bring everything in these thick, square glass bottles instead of the usual plastic handles. The distillery is up in the mountains, run by these two brothers who used to be aerospace engineers or something. They built their own copper stills completely from scratch. They use juniper, obviously, but they also throw in spruce tips that they forage themselves. It gives the gin this really intense piney taste, almost like you're drinking a Christmas tree. People either absolutely love it or they send it back immediately. They brought in a sample batch last year in these little custom wooden crates with their logo burned into the side. They even stamp each batch number by hand on the label with this old-school red ink...

At all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don't know if sad is the right word for it. I'm not really sure. My wife actually asked me the exact same thing yesterday when she came home with this new sweater for me. She said the color was supposed to cheer me up or something. It's this bright blue... and I haven't worn anything like that since the new owners made us switch our uniforms at work. \n\nDid I mention the new uniforms? They've got us in these stiff, black button-downs now with their new logo embroidered right on the chest. The collar is so stiff it practically chokes you, and the fabric doesn't breathe at all. I mean, you're running around on a busy Friday night, hauling thirty-pound ice buckets from the back, and you're just sweating straight through this cheap polyester. The old owner, Mike, he used to just let us wear whatever black t-shirt we wanted as long as it didn't have holes in it and was clean. Made it so much easier to actually move around and do the job. You can't reach up to the top shelf for the good bourbon in these new shirts without feeling like you're gonna rip a seam. \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, sad. Yeah, I don't really know about sad. I just haven't really noticed feeling much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure when you're doing things, even if it's just a little bit?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're just going in circles here. I mean, I told you, right? I came in because nothing feels like mine anymore. I used to actually get excited about things. The murals, going for runs, playing basketball... even just making a really good drink at work. Now I just stand there and it's like I don't care about any of it. \n\nSpeaking of not caring, the delivery guy today, he just dumped all the new liquor boxes right in the middle of the hallway. Didn't even bother stacking them against the wall. I almost tripped over a case of vodka on my way to the cooler. And the boxes are getting cheaper, too. The cardboard is so thin now that if you try to pick them up by the sides, the bottom just falls right out. We lost three bottles of gin last week because of that. Just shattered everywhere. The smell of juniper was stuck in the floor mats for three days, no matter how many times I mopped it with the heavy-duty degreaser. We used to get the good citrus degreaser, the one that actually cut through the stickiness, but now they buy this cheap industrial stuff that just smells like bleach and gives me a headache. \n\nBut yeah... no pleasure. None at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don't know exactly how much weight, but yeah, my pants are definitely looser. Most days I just forget to eat entirely until my hands start shaking while I'm trying to pour a drink behind the bar. Then maybe I'll grab a few bites of whatever cold fries or something are left after we close, but even just looking at it makes me feel sick. \n\nWhich is a shame, because the kitchen staff, well, the old kitchen staff, they used to make this incredible family meal before service. Carlos, the old head chef, he'd do these slow-roasted pork tacos on Thursdays. The smell would just fill the whole prep area. He'd marinate the pork for like two days in this citrus and achiote blend. But Carlos left when the new owners took over. Said they were trying to cut his food costs so much he couldn't even buy decent tomatoes anymore. Now they've got this guy, Greg, running the line. Greg doesn't even taste the food before it goes out. I saw him send out a plate of wings the other night that were practically still frozen in the middle. And he's always blasting this terrible techno music from a little Bluetooth speaker right next to the fryers. You can hear it all the way out in the dining room when the kitchen doors swing open. It totally ruins the vibe. We used to just have low-key jazz or something playing, you know? Kept people relaxed. \n\nBut yeah, I haven't really been eating much. Just don't have the stomach for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... yeah, I do. All the time, honestly. I keep thinking about how I let down the younger bartenders. And the artists, too. I promised them I'd secure some wall space for their murals before the new owners came in, and I just... I didn't get it done in time. Now they have nowhere to show their work. I wrecked their shot, and it just feels like I deserve whatever happens to me because of it. \n\nSpeaking of the murals, you should have seen what this one kid, Leo, was working on. He had these massive canvases in his garage. He uses this technique where he mixes sand into the paint to give it texture, and he was doing this whole series on the history of the neighborhood. The colors were just unbelievable. Really vibrant reds and these deep, rich ochres. He said he had to order the pigments special from some supplier in New York. I remember he was telling me about how the shipping costs were killing him, but he refused to compromise on the quality. That's the thing about these young artists, they just pour everything they have into it. Leo was working two jobs just to afford the materials. He'd come into the bar after his shift at the warehouse, completely exhausted, with paint still stuck under his fingernails, and just talk for hours about color theory and composition. I used to give him a free soda and just listen. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... I just feel like it's all my fault. I really let them down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: No, no, I haven't been thinking about hurting myself. I just... I don't know, I just feel empty, mostly. Not like I want to do anything bad to myself. \n\nThough, you know, it is dangerous behind the bar sometimes. People don't realize. Just last month, before I really started feeling this way, we had this guy come in, completely out of his mind. He was swinging a pool cue around because someone bumped into him near the jukebox. The jukebox is this vintage Wurlitzer, beautiful machine, it lights up with all these neon tubes, pinks and greens. The owner bought it at an auction in Chicago, paid a fortune to have it shipped down here and restored. It still takes actual quarters, none of that digital app nonsense. You have to physically push the heavy metal buttons, and you can hear the mechanism whirring and clanking as it grabs the record. Anyway, this guy swings the cue and smashes right into the glass of the Wurlitzer. Glass went everywhere, all over the floor, all over the guy who bumped him. I had to jump over the bar and physically tackle the guy with the cue before he hit someone. My knee still aches from where I landed on the brass footrail. \n\nBut no... I'm not thinking about hurting myself. Just... just tired.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've lost interest in almost all activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I guess so. I actually came in because nothing feels like mine anymore, you know? I used to get so excited about things. Helping the kids with their murals, going for runs in the morning, playing pick-up basketball on Sundays... hell, even just making a really good, balanced cocktail used to give me a little thrill. Now I just stand behind the bar and I don't care about any of it. \n\nSpeaking of basketball, the courts over at the park are just falling apart. It's a shame. The city was supposed to resurface them last spring, but they just patched a few of the worst cracks with this cheap asphalt filler that gets all sticky when the sun beats down on it. And the nets! They haven't replaced the nets in years. Half of them are just hanging by a single loop of chain, clanking against the rim every time the wind blows. I remember back in the day, we used to have these huge tournaments out there. Teams from all over the city would show up. There was this one guy, Marcus, he was maybe six-foot-four, played D2 ball somewhere up state. He had this fadeaway jumper that was just impossible to block. He'd always wear these bright neon green sneakers, said they distracted the defense. I don't know if it worked, but nobody could guard him. We used to play until the streetlights came on, and then we'd all head over to the corner bodega for Gatorades and cheap pizza. \n\nBut yeah... I just don't have the energy or the care for any of it anymore. I just feel totally disconnected from it all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant changes in your sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep is... it's really messed up right now. I get home and I'm exhausted, you know? Just completely drained. So I lie down, but my head just starts spinning. It just keeps going over every single mistake I made at work, every little thing I messed up. And then when I finally do pass out, I'm wide awake by four or five in the morning and I just can't get back to sleep. \n\nIt's so quiet at that time of morning. I usually end up just staring out the window. You can see the bakery across the street starting up their ovens. They get their flour deliveries right around 4:30. This massive truck pulls up—it's one of those big eighteen-wheelers, always has this loud, screeching air brake that echoes off all the buildings. The driver is this older guy, must be in his sixties, always wears a faded Mets cap. He unloads these huge fifty-pound sacks of flour on a hand truck, just stacks them up on the sidewalk before hauling them inside. Sometimes the bakery owner comes out and hands him a coffee and one of those fresh croissants they make. They make the best almond croissants there, really flaky, with powdered sugar all over the top. I used to go over there and grab one before my morning run, back when I actually did that. The smell of the butter and the yeast just fills the whole street before the sun even comes up. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I lie there and can't go back to sleep. It's miserable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have less energy than you used to?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely. It's like I have absolutely nothing left in the tank. They use juniper, obviously, but they also throw in spruce tips that they forage themselves. It gives the gin this really intense piney taste, almost like you're drinking a Christmas tree. Yeah, I mean... I think I said this earlier, but yeah. Nothing feels like mine anymore. It's like I'm just going through the motions every single day. I used to actually care, you know? Like I'd get genuinely excited to help the kids in the neighborhood paint those big murals on the brick walls down by the community center. Or going for my runs, playing basketball... even just making a really solid, well-balanced drink at work used to give me this little rush. Now I just stand behind the bar and I feel absolutely nothing. 

Speaking of the bar, we actually just got this new espresso machine installed last week. It's this massive Italian thing, shiny chrome all over, looks like the engine of a vintage sports car. The owner spent a fortune on it because he wants to start doing these high-end coffee cocktails. The guy who came to install it was telling me about the pressure valves and how it regulates the steam... he had this thick accent, said his family has been building these machines in Florence for three generations. He even brought in these special beans that are roasted over olive wood. Supposedly it gives the espresso this really subtle, smoky finish that pairs perfectly with dark rum. I had to spend three hours learning how to properly tamp the grounds so the water pulls through at the exact right speed. If you pack it too tight, it just drips out like molasses and gets all bitter. 

But yeah... whether I'm pulling a perfect shot or pouring a beer, it doesn't matter. I just don't care about any of it anymore. No pleasure in it at all.
